<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for astrological compatibility, transitioning from the classification of castes into the specific "hostilities" between animal types and the planetary rulership of the zodiac signs, leading into the Temperament Match (Gana Kuta) and Sign Match (Rashi Kuta).</meta>

The **Monkey** and the **Cow-Tiger** pairs are described as having natural enmity, as is the **Great Serpent** <note>original: *mahahim*; referring to the mongoose-snake or similar predator-prey relationship</note>. Regarding those whose nature is to be "eaten" and those who are "eaters," there is a natural hostility between them. 

Now, the lords of the signs beginning with Aries are: **Mars** <note>original: *Bhauma*</note>, **Venus** <note>original: *Shukra*</note>, **Mercury** <note>original: *Budha*</note>, the **Moon** <note>original: *Indu*</note>, the **Sun** <note>original: *Bhanu*</note>, and the lords of the two signs belonging to **Saturn** <note>original: *Shani*</note>, as well as **Jupiter** <note>original: *Jiva*</note>. Regarding the Sun, its friends are said to be the Moon and others, while its enemies are the opposite. For the Moon, there are no actual enemies, though Mercury and the Sun are considered neutral or equal. For Mars, Venus and Saturn are neutral, while Mercury is an enemy and the rest are friends. For Mercury, the Moon is an enemy while the others are friends or neutral. For Jupiter, Saturn is an enemy while the Sun is a friend and the others are medium or best. For Venus <note>original: *Bhrigu*</note>, Jupiter and Mars are neutral, while Mercury and Saturn are friends and the others are enemies. For Saturn, Jupiter is neutral, while Venus and Mercury are friends and the rest are enemies. This mutual friendship in the divisional charts is considered best or medium; if they are indifferent, it is considered the lowest status. This calculation is the very "breath and soul" of the matching process.

<insert>Now the Temperament Match</insert> <note>original: *Gana Kuta*; a method of matching the psychological temperaments of the couple categorized as Divine, Human, or Demonic</note>

According to the *Patalsara*, in all matters, there should be affection and friendship between the **Divine** <note>original: *Deva*</note> and **Human** <note>original: *Nara*</note> temperaments. However, there is natural enmity between the **Demonic** <note>original: *Asura*</note> and the Divine. The **Human** temperament is considered best for marriage with its own kind. If the bride is of the **Human** group <note>original: *Manavi*</note>, some allow the marriage to proceed, while others here do not desire it. If the bride is of the **Demonic** group <note>original: *Rakshasa*</note> and the groom is of the **Human** group, it is said that in the eighth month after the wedding, she "consumes the flesh of her husband" <note>This is a traditional astrological metaphor for the wife causing the husband's downfall or early death due to incompatible temperaments</note>. However, when the groom is of the **Demonic** group and the bride is **Human**, the marriage is proclaimed as auspicious by the sages **Vashistha**, **Atri**, and **Parashara**.

Now, regarding the **Sign Match** <note>original: *Rashi Kuta*</note>, the sage **Narada** says: If the signs are in the sixth and eighth positions from each other, it leads to death; if in the fifth and ninth, it leads to the loss of children. If they are in the second and twelfth positions, it causes poverty, but in other positions, the affection between the couple is excellent. According to the text *Jyotiprakasha*, if they share the **same sign**, there is great affection. If they are in the fourth and tenth positions, they are like friends. In the third and eleventh positions, they gain wealth. If in the seventh position, they will have offspring. According to the *Jyotirnibandha*, if the **Lunar Mansion** <gloss>original: *Nakshatra*</gloss> is the same...

If the bride and groom belong to different zodiac signs, or if they belong to the same sign, the marriage is considered. If they share the same sign but have different constellations <note>original: *Nakshatras*; the 27 lunar mansions used in Indian astrology</note>, then the marriage brings happiness. As the sage **Shaunaka** says: If the couple has the same zodiac sign but different constellations, then the faults of temperament <note>original: *Gana-dosha*</note> and the pulse-match fault <note>original: *Nadi-dosha*</note> are nullified, and the marriage is remembered as auspicious. In the text **The Light of Astrology** <term>Jyotih-Prakasha</term>, it is stated that if the couple shares the same sign and the same quarter <note>original: *pada*; each constellation is divided into four quarters</note>, the marriage grants the blessing of sons and grandsons. 

The constellations associated with the **First Pulse** <note>original: *Aadi Nadi*</note> are: Ashvini, Ardra, Punarvasu, Uttara Phalguni, Hasta, Jyeshtha, Mula, Purva Bhadrapada, and Shatabhisha. 

The constellations of the **Second Pulse** <note>original: *Madhya Nadi*</note> are: Bharani, Mrigashira, Pushya, Purva Phalguni, Chitra, Anuradha, Purvashadha, Dhanishta, and Uttara Bhadrapada. 

The constellations of the **Third Pulse** <note>original: *Antya Nadi*</note> are: Krittika, Rohini, Ashlesha, Magha, Swati, Vishakha, Shravana, Dhanishta <note>Dhanishta and others sometimes overlap in different traditions' classifications</note>, and Revati. These are the pulses where a death-like state <note>original: *mruta*</note> is avoided in marriage.

The great astrologer **Varahamihira** <term>Varaha</term> states: If both partners share the **First Pulse**, it causes separation; if both share the **Middle Pulse**, it leads to the destruction of both; if both share the **Last Pulse**, it results in widowhood and extreme sorrow. Therefore, one must strictly avoid a match where the pulses are identical. 

However, there is an exception to this: for the constellations Rohini, Ardra, Mrigashira, Pushya, Shravana, and Revati, the <term>Nadi-dosha</term> <note>the specific astrological "fault" or incompatibility arising from having the same pulse</note> does not apply. These are the rules for the matching clusters.

Now, regarding the selection of the bride: the three "Purva" constellations <note>Purva Phalguni, Purvashadha, Purva Bhadrapada</note>, Shravana, Anuradha, and the constellations of the Universal Gods <note>original: *Vaishvadeva*; referring to Uttara Ashadha</note> are preferred. **Lord Maheshwara** <note>an epithet of Shiva</note> says: if malefic planets are placed in the 3rd, 6th, and 11th houses, and benefic planets occupy the 7th and 9th houses or the angles <note>original: *kendras*; the 1st, 4th, 7th, and 10th houses</note>, or if Jupiter is in an increasing position, the groom should choose the maiden. 

Now, concerning the conduct for choosing the groom: the three "Purva" constellations, and the constellations of the fire...

...for three nights. Likewise, for the bride and groom, Mars <note>original: *Mangala*; associated with the constellation Rohini's son in certain mythic contexts</note> brings success. In the sixth month, various fruits should be given to the bride and groom by the girl's brother or another relative. 

A girl should not be given in marriage to one who is too closely related, nor to one who is extremely weak, lacking a livelihood, or to a widower. According to **The Moonlight of Tradition** <term>Smriti Chandrika; a 12th-century legal digest</term>, if a death is certain within the family, or if a death of a kinsman <note>original: *Gotraja*; a member of the same paternal lineage</note> occurs after the marriage is fixed, it creates an obstacle. The performance of auspicious ceremonies like garden-consecrations while in mourning is said to bring the risk of widowhood and grief. 

If a death occurs in either family after the formal "giving of the word" <note>original: *Vardana/Vagdana*; the formal engagement</note>, the marriage will be auspicious if delayed:
* If it is the father who has died, wait for half a year.
* If it is the mother, wait for half that time <note>three months</note>.
* For a wife, wait three months.
* For a brother or a son, wait half of that time <note>one and a half months</note>.

There is a special rule regarding the "setting of the gods" <note>likely referring to the period when deities are believed to sleep, *Deva-shayana*</note>: if there is an obstacle regarding a blood relative <term>Sapinda</term>, one should avoid the marriage for one month. After that, the wedding may be performed. However, if the girl has reached maturity or if there is a danger to the parents' lives, the marriage is not forbidden despite these obstacles.

### The Proper Age for a Maiden’s Marriage

Now, regarding the time for a girl’s marriage:
Within the first six years, the marriage is most auspicious. For the first two years, the girl is said to be enjoyed by the moon <note>original: *Soma*; the moon is the first celestial guardian of a girl's development</note>, then by the celestial musicians <note>original: *Gandharvas*</note>, and thereafter by the fire god <note>original: *Hutashana/Agni*</note>. 

According to the sage **Kashyapa**: for women, a marriage in an even year from birth grants sons and grandsons; in an odd year, it grants daughters. For men, the opposite is true.

According to the **Brahma Purana**:
* At eight years old, she is **Gauri** <note>named after the "shining" goddess, representing pre-pubescent purity</note>.
* At nine years old, she is **Rohini**.
* At ten years old, she is a **Maiden** <note>original: *Kanya*</note>.
* At twelve years old, she is remembered as **Vrishali** <note>original: *Vrishali*; a term used here to mean "ritually impure" due to the onset of menstruation</note>.

Once she passes ten years, the girl is considered to have lost her ritual purity. Therefore, a father should not delay the "grasping of the hand" <note>original: *Panigrahana*; the wedding ceremony</note> beyond that age. If he does not give the girl in marriage by her twelfth year, then every month...

If a girl reaches menstruation <note>original: *Rajasvalā*; referring to the onset of puberty</note> [while still residing in her father’s house], her father, grandfather, brother, paternal uncle, and maternal uncle—all five of these men are said to go to hell.

Regarding a girl who has reached maturity, the sage Narada states that she must be purified by the month. The months of Magha <note>January/February</note>, Phalguna <note>February/March</note>, Vaishakha <note>April/May</note>, and Jyeshtha <note>May/June</note> are those that grant auspiciousness. The months of Kartika and Margashirsha are considered average, while the others are censured. According to the **Gem of the Tonsure** <term>Chūḍāratna; a treatise on rituals</term>, some say marriage can occur in the month of Pausha when the Sun is in Capricorn, or in Chaitra when the Sun is in Aries, or even in Ashadha when the Sun is in Gemini. Narada further specifies: a marriage should not take place in the birth month of the individuals, nor under their birth star <term>Nakshatra</term>, nor on their actual birthday. Furthermore, a wedding should not be held if the mother of either the bride or groom is currently pregnant. Guru <note>the planet Jupiter or the preceptor Brihaspati</note> advises that regarding the marriage of the eldest child <note>original: *Jyeshtha*; meaning both "eldest" and the name of a month/star</note>, if only one of the pair is the eldest, it may proceed <unclear>even then</unclear>. **Varaha** states: If two "eldest" factors are present, it is considered middling; if only one is present, it is auspicious. However, one should never perform a marriage during the "Triad of Jyeshtha" <note>a traditionally forbidden combination where the eldest child marries in the month of Jyeshtha under the star Jyeshtha</note>, according to the consensus of all authorities. **Bharadvaja** offers an exception: one may marry the eldest in the month of Jyeshtha only if the Sun is in a specific position, but festivals and <unclear>certain activities</unclear> should still be avoided. **Vasishtha** advises on the lunar days <term>Tithis</term>: in the bright fortnight, the second day and others <unclear>are spoken of as</unclear> auspicious until the tenth. However, the eighth, the sixth, and the fourteenth days should be avoided. Regarding the days of the week <term>Vara</term>: the days of the benefic planets are praised, while the Sun and Saturn are only middling. The day of the son of Earth <note>original: *Bhumisuta*; the planet Mars</note> must always be avoided. One must also avoid the twilight hours <term>Pradosha</term> on the lunar dates of the Sun and Moon. Regarding the purity of the stars: the three "fixed" stars, the "friendly" star <note>Maitra/Anuradha</note>, and those ruled by the Wind and Fire are always praised for the taking of a maiden's hand, provided they are free from other astrological defects. **Now, the procedure for the altar and other matters:**

Narada says: [To construct the diagram], draw two horizontal and two vertical lines, and two more in the corners. In the second "Corner of Shiva" <note>the northeast corner</note>, one should place the **Star-Cycle** <term>Dhishṇya-cakra; a specialized astrological chart used to visualize planetary influences on lunar mansions</term>.

Since that [diagram] includes the star Abhijit <note>the "intercalary" 28th star often used in specific rituals</note>, it is arranged accordingly. **Vasishtha** states: On this chart, once [the stars] are positioned, the movement [of planets] is observed. It is declared that a "piercing" <note>original: *viddha*; an affliction or negative aspect</note> by malefic planets affects the whole mansion, while the influence of benefic planets is cut off if they do not occupy even a single quarter of the star.

Now, regarding the **Planetary Obstruction** <term>Avagraha; a specific type of astrological affliction where a planet's position blocks the auspiciousness of a star</term>: when the Sun is in the twelfth star [from the moon], Jupiter and Saturn in the sixth, or Mars <note>original: *nṛja*; likely a scribal variant for *kuja* (Mars)</note> in the third or seventh, it is unfavorable. The Sun <note>original: *Divākara*</note> also afflicts the eighth star ahead and the twentieth star behind.

On the day of the Full Moon <note>original: *Pūrṇimā*</note>, the fifth star is auspicious. Regarding the **Fault of the Falling Node** <term>Pāta-doṣa; a period when the sun and moon have equal declination, considered highly inauspicious</term>, **Trivikrama** says: For those who strictly observe the sightings [of the heavenly bodies], the yogas of **Vaidhriti** and **Vyatipata** <note>two of the twenty-seven astrological "conjunctions," both considered ill-omened</note> are significant. If a limb [of the ceremony] occurs during these "falls," the merit is struck down.

Regarding **Secondary Faults** <note>original: *avāntara-doṣa*</note>, Vasishtha says: Excellence [is found] before or after the Vyatipata yoga; however, if the ritual occurs while the influence is fierce, it is struck down. In the stars of Chitra, Anuradha, Magha <note>original: *Pitṛ*</note>, and Ashlesha <note>original: *Pannaga*</note>, as well as those belonging to the Sun, these are sensitive points. When drawing the lines, seven horizontal and [seven] vertical, if the Sun’s orb <note>original: *maṇḍala*</note> falls upon the intersection, it is a specific type of fault.

Now, he states the **Burnt Combinations** <term>Dagdha-yoga; literally "burnt union," referring to specific dates where the combination of the weekday and lunar star is considered destructive</term>: In whatever star the Sun resides, and whichever fault-removing [remedy] is applied, if the sum of the two is multiplied and results in thirteen, fifteen, or eleven, or if it is less than twenty... these are proclaimed as Burnt Yogas. [The results of such a timing include] fear of kings, thieves, death, disease, thunderbolts, and the loss of harmony.

## Now Narada speaks on the Fault of Conjunction
<term>Yuti-doṣa; an affliction caused when two or more planets occupy the same zodiacal space</term>. 

When malefic planets <note>original: *pāpā*; refers to naturally harsh planets like Mars or Saturn</note> are conjoined with Ketu <note>the South Node of the Moon</note>, there are seven types of faults. If the Sun is conjoined with Rahu <note>original: *la*; likely an abbreviation for *Rāhu*, the North Node</note>, it certainly leads to poverty. A conjunction with a "cruel" planet <note>original: *krūreṇa*; a malefic</note> causes death or disease. Through a conjunction with a benefic planet <note>original: *saumyena*</note>, there is a lack of children. 

If Jupiter and Venus are in the same part, it leads to the life of a wandering ascetic <term>Pravrajyā; the renunciation of worldly life, which would be considered a "fault" in the context of planning a marriage</term>. If all planets are conjoined, it leads to kings, wars, and constant strife. If conjoined with Ketu, there is constant hardship and poverty. 

## Now regarding the Jamitra Fault from the *Bhujabala*
<term>Jāmitra-doṣa; an affliction related to the 7th house of the horoscope, which governs marriage and partners</term> <note>The *Bhujabala* refers to the *Bhujabala-bhīma*, a classic astrological treatise</note>.

If the Sun <note>original: *Dinakara*</note> is in the seventh house, the girl is abandoned by her relatives. With Mars <note>original: *Bhauma*</note> in the seventh, the woman meets her end. With Saturn <note>original: *Saura*</note>, she becomes barren. Jupiter, Venus, and Mercury <note>original: *Śaśāṅka-ja*, literally "Moon's son"</note> are auspicious in this position, according to some authorities. In other planetary combinations in the seventh house, she will be someone who is constantly traveling or living abroad.

## Now Narada speaks on the Fault of Secondary Planets
<term>Upagraha-doṣa; afflictions from mathematically derived points or minor celestial bodies</term>. 

An earthquake [is predicted] when certain points are in the seventh, eighth, or ninth house from the Sun’s star. If Ketu is in the fifth, there is "punishment" <note>original: *daṇḍa*</note>. If there is a "thunderstroke" <term>Nirghāta; a sudden atmospheric or celestial disturbance</term> or a falling meteor <note>original: *pāta*</note>... [the text mentions counts of 18, 20, and 21 for these various omens]. These omens, including halos around the sun or moon <term>Pariveṣa</term>, must be understood in their proper sequence beginning from the twenty-first star.

## Now regarding the Five-fold Fault
<term>Pañcaka-doṣa; a method of numerology used to determine if a specific time is safe for travel or ritual</term>. 

For a marriage, take the elapsed lunar day <term>Tithi</term> and the rising sign <term>Lagna</term>, and arrange them in five places. These are then divided by certain numbers [to find the remainder]. According to the remainder, the results are: 
1. Death of a child
2. Disease <note>original: *rugna*</note>
3. Fire/Heat <note>original: *anala*</note>
4. Theft <note>original: *caura*</note>
5. Death <note>original: *mṛtyu*</note>

## Now the Astrologer speaks on the [Nine-fold] Fault

Take the Lunar Day, the Day of the Week, and the Birth Star <term>Nakṣatra</term>, multiply by nine, and divide by seven. From the remainder, one should predict the results. <note>The text repeats this formula for emphasis</note>. If the remainder is [specific numbers], it leads to knowledge/success, but a remainder of five leads to...

...eating in the seventh house, or [omens relating to] kings; these are known as the signs of kings. Such are the **Ten Great Faults**.

## Purification of the Ascendant according to Trivikrama
<term>Lagna Shuddhi; the process of ensuring the rising sign at the moment of a wedding is free from planetary afflictions</term> <note>Trivikrama is a celebrated historical authority on Hindu astrology</note>.

One should abandon a Lagna <note>Ascendant</note> if Saturn <note>original: *Manda*</note> is in the Lagna and Venus <note>original: *Shukra*</note> is in the 12th house <note>the house of loss and expenditure</note>. Similarly, avoid the Lagna if planets occupy the 8th house <note>original: *randhre*; the house of death</note>. These five are to be avoided. Malefic planets <note>original: *pāpa*</note> should not be in the 5th house. 

The Moon <note>original: *Indu*</note> and Venus should be avoided in the Lagna itself. Elsewhere, the Sun <note>original: *Dinakara*</note> is beneficial in the 11th house <note>the house of gains</note>. Mars <note>original: *Bhauma*</note> is good in the 3rd, 6th, and 11th houses. Mercury <note>original: *Budha*</note> and Jupiter <note>original: *Guru*</note> are auspicious in all houses except the 12th, 8th, and 7th. Venus <note>original: *Shukra*</note> is forbidden in the 12th, 6th, 7th, and 8th. The Sun is especially harmful in the 7th house, while Mercury in the Lagna is considered censurable for marriage, though it may be good for other rituals.

## Characteristics of the Godhuli Timing
<term>Godhūli; literally "cow-dust," referring to the time of sunset when cows return from the forest, kicking up dust with their hooves</term>.

When a perfectly purified Ascendant is not available, the wise sages say that the **Godhuli** time is beneficial. If a pure and powerful Lagna is available, then the Godhuli time does not provide additional results. In this Kali Yuga <note>the current historical age in Hindu cosmology</note>, the Godhuli time is praised by astrologers as a means to balance faults. However, if malefic planets are in the 1st, 7th, or 8th houses, even Godhuli must be abandoned. Other minor faults are nullified by its auspiciousness.

The dust that is raised from the earth by the hard hooves of cows returning [at sunset] gives various kinds of wealth, children, and health to women at the time of their marriage. 

> At this time, no [fault of] the lunar day <term>Tithi</term>, the half-day <term>Karaṇa</term>, the Ascendant, or the planetary combinations <term>Yoga</term> applies. It is well known to settle all of a man's business and destroy sins through the sacred dust of the cows.

As long as the sun's orb is visible or its rays are seen in the sky, that duration of the "cow-dust" is recognized by the wise as a powerful Lagna. However, it should not be used on Saturdays <note>original: *Manda-vāre*</note>, nor when the Moon is in the 6th or 8th house. According to some authorities, Godhuli is especially effective when Saturn is in the 11th, 2nd, or 3rd house. All other positions are considered ordinary. This timing is not to be used during the solar months of Cancer and others, nor during specific lunar phases...

...and in the auspicious solar half-division, with Jupiter <note>original: *Devajīva*</note> in the Ascendant <note>original: *Lagna*</note>, the wedding is considered pure and brings excellence to the bride's family.

### The Commencement of Marriage Rituals
<term>Vivāha-kṛtya-ārambha; the beginning of the specific ceremonial acts of the wedding</term>

One should observe the entire marriage rite within a suitable marriage Nakshatra <note>Lunar Mansion</note>, also ensuring the strength of the Moon <note>original: *Himadyuti*; literally "the cool-rayed one"</note>. According to the tradition of the ancestors, the auspicious anointing with oil <note>original: *taila-maṅgalam*</note> should be performed first for the groom. This ritual should be carried out as previously specified in the texts. According to the *Muhūrta-Cintāmaṇi*, it is pleasing to the astrologer to perform this on an odd-numbered day when the Moon is auspicious, and at night. 

The Lunar Mansions of Citrā, Viśākhā, Śatatarakā, Aśvinī, Jyeṣṭhā, and Bharaṇī are considered appropriate for the primary anointing <note>original: *tasta-phala-taila*</note> and the preparation of the wedding altar <note>original: *vedikā*</note>, as well as for the construction of the pavilion <note>original: *maṇḍapa*</note>.

### The Ritual of Wearing the Girdle
<term>Kāñcodhāraṇa; the ceremonial putting on of the sacred girdle or waist-cloth</term>

The rituals of installation and wearing should be performed under the Lunar Mansions of Mūla, Śravaṇā, Arka (Hastā), Puṣya, Citrā, Anurādhā, and Revatī. This should take place in the ritual water vessel <note>original: *kuṇḍikā*</note> on the days of the Sun (Sunday) or Mars (Tuesday). 

### The Ritual of Medhana
<term>Medhana-dhāraṇa; a rite associated with the sacred thread or girdle, signifying intelligence and memory</term>

In the Lunar Mansions of Mṛgaśīrṣā, Anurādhā, and Varuṇa (Śatabhiṣā), and on the days associated with the Sun and the three malefic planets, one achieves success in the installation. This is certain to be successful when the Ascendant is aspected <note>viewed</note> by Venus <note>original: *Kavi*</note> and occurs in the auspicious twelfth division.

<insert>This concludes the Fourth Chapter on the Marriage Section in the *Muhūrta-darpaṇa* (The Mirror of Auspicious Moments).</insert>

### The Entry of the New Bride
<term>Nava-vadhū-praveśa; the ritualized first entry of the bride into her husband’s home</term>

According to the *Jyotir-nibandha* <note>a classical compendium on astrology</note>, the entry of the bride after the marriage should take place on an even-numbered day, specifically after sixteen days have passed. If it is beyond that, it should be on the fifth day or any other prescribed day. There is no strict rule regarding the years, however, if a woman enters the home in an even year and an even month, it is said that she may steal away the lifespan of her husband or the woman herself may meet her end. The entry of the bride at the "fruit-bearing" time <note>original: *phalodaya*; perhaps referring to pregnancy or a specific stage of the cycle</note> is spoken of as a religious duty for men. This is as specified by the sages such as Parāśara.

### Purity of the Lunar Mansions for Entry
<term>Nakṣatra-śuddhi; ensuring the Moon is in a favorable constellation for the bride's arrival</term>

According to the *Yavāra-dvārā* system, the constellations of Revatī <note>original: *Pauṣṇa*</note>, Aśvinī, the two Śravaṇās, Mūla, and the "Uttara" (latter) mansions, as well as the sixth and the mansion of Mitra (Anurādhā) are auspicious for the bride...

### The Rite of the Bride’s Entry
<term>Vadhūpraveśa; the ceremonial first entry of the bride into her husband’s house</term>

The entry is considered auspicious on the days of Jupiter <note>original: *Ijya*</note>, the Sun <note>original: *Arka*</note>, and Mars <note>original: *Kuja*</note>, but one must avoid the "Empty" <note>original: *Riktā*; the 4th, 9th, and 14th lunar days</note> and "Bloody" days. If she enters the home when the Sun is dominant, wealth is attained; if under Mars, it leads to hardship. If under Mercury, she will be blessed with sons. However, under Jupiter, Venus, Saturn, or the Moon, the bride may not bring prosperity <note>This refers to specific complex planetary combinations where these otherwise "soft" planets might be poorly placed</note>.

If a maiden enters her husband’s house during the wedding festivities or immediately after: 
If she enters at an improper time, she may destroy her mother; if at another time, her own body; if at a specific late hour, she becomes a source of contentment for her father. If she enters during a "clouded" <note>original: *Ghoṣe*</note> time, she may bring harm to her father-in-law or husband. In the month of Chaitra <note>March-April</note> or during an intercalary month <note>original: *Malimlucha*; an extra month added to the lunar calendar to sync with the solar year, generally considered inauspicious for rituals</note>, the entry destroys the father or brings great fear. 

->Thus concludes the teaching from the **Rājamārttaṇḍa**.<-

### On Departing for the Husband’s Home
<term>Abhigamana; the journey or departure toward the husband's residence</term>

Now, concerning the departure to the husband’s house: it is auspicious when the Sun is in a favorable transit and when Venus <note>original: *Bhārgava*</note> is not combust <note>original: *Asangate*; when a planet is too close to the sun to be seen</note>. 

The Sun should ideally be in the signs of Scorpio, Aquarius, or Aries <note>original: *Kīṭa-Ghaṭa-Aja*</note>. The departure should occur during the bright fortnight <note>original: *Kṛṣṇetare*; literally "other than the dark fortnight"</note>. If Mercury and Venus are in the 2nd house or other gainful positions, it is highly beneficial. 

The Lunar Mansions <note>original: *Tārā*</note> of Śravaṇā, Rohiṇī, Puṣya, and Revatī are auspicious for wearing new clothes and for the daily festivities within the home. The Lunar Mansions of Maghā, Mūla, and the three Uttaras <note>Uttara Phalguni, Uttara Ashadha, and Uttara Bhadrapada</note> are also praised for the young bride's journey. According to the sage **Bādarāyaṇa**, the departure should be done on a "gentle" day <note>Monday, Wednesday, Thursday, or Friday</note> when the Sun is pure. 

However, one must never perform the departure of a newly married woman in the months of Chaitra or Pausa, nor during an intercalary month. If she departs during these times, she may face death within five months.

### The Flaw of Venus’s Direction
<term>Śukra-mukha-doṣa; the astrological flaw caused by traveling in the direction where Venus is positioned</term>

When Venus <note>original: *Bhṛgu*</note> or Jupiter <note>original: *Jīva*</note> is set <note>combust</note>, one should never move the bride. Even a child or a newlywed should not take a single step toward the direction of Venus. 

**Exceptions to the Venus Flaw:**
One does not need to consider the position of Venus in the following circumstances:
1. Within the same village or city.
2. During a national uprising or revolution <note>original: *Rāṣṭra-viplave*</note>.
3. During a marriage ceremony itself.
4. While on a pilgrimage to a holy site <note>original: *Tīrthayātrā*</note>.
5. When protected by the strength of one’s own lineage <note>original: *Gotra-rakṣaṇā*</note>.

It emphasizes avoiding intercalary months and the "combust" states of Venus and Jupiter, while noting exceptions for emergencies and pilgrimages.</summary>

Regarding this, it is said: For those of the Kashyapa, Agni, Bharadvaja, and Vatsa lineages <note>original: *gotras*</note>, "Facing Venus" <note>original: *Pratishukra*; a taboo against traveling in the direction where the planet Venus is currently positioned in the sky</note> does not cause any harm. Sage Kashyapa adds: For the descendants of Pulastya, Vashishtha, and Atri, Venus is considered favorable even when encountered head-on during a journey intended for a beneficial purpose. In cases of traveling to a different country, during times of great confusion, during weddings, or upon the first arrival of a new bride, the consideration of "Facing Venus" is not required. The sage Bhima states: Within the same village, within a four-pillared house, or when a woman is being led away by force, "Facing Venus" does not result in any blemish. **Thus ends the chapter on Returning Home in the "Mirror of Auspicious Moments" <note>original: *Muhurta-Darpana*</note>.**

### Now, the First Topic

Traveling is auspicious under a masculine planetary alignment in the following lunar mansions: the three "Uttaras" <note>Uttara Phalguni, Uttara Ashadha, and Uttara Bhadrapada</note>, Rohini, Mrigashira, Anuradha, and Hasta; as well as Chitra, Swati, and Vishakha. When Virgo is the rising sign or during a marriage ritual, travel is successful if benefics <note>planets like Jupiter or Venus</note> are in the "Angles" <note>the 1st, 4th, 7th, or 10th houses of a birth chart</note>, and malefics <note>planets like Saturn or Mars</note> are in the 3rd, 6th, or 11th houses, provided they are aspected by auspicious planets. It should be done on a gentle day <note>Monday, Wednesday, Thursday, or Friday</note>, during a favorable combination of astronomical factors <note>original: *yoga* and *karana*</note>, avoiding the "Empty" <note>the 4th, 9th, and 14th lunar days</note> or the eighth lunar day. For those who lack these auspicious combinations, a neutral alignment is acceptable.

As stated in the "Lamp of Fire-Establishment" <note>original: *Agnyadhana-Dipika*</note>: One should establish the ritual fire under the mansions of Rohini, the three Uttaras, or Vishakha, especially when they coincide with an auspicious solar transition. According to Shri-pati, for the Rite of Establishing the Sacred Fire <note>term: *Agnyadhana*; the formal ceremony of lighting the three domestic or sacrificial fires</note>, the Moon and Sun should be strong. When the Sun is in the 1st, 4th, 5th, 7th, 9th, or 10th house, and the Moon is in an upachaya house <note>the 3rd, 6th, 10th, or 11th houses, associated with growth</note>, and other planets are well-placed, it generates auspicious wealth. 

In the "Compendium of Astrology" <note>original: *Jyotir-Nibandha*</note>: A twice-born <note>a member of the three upper castes</note> should perform the Laksha-homa <note>a sacrifice of 100,000 oblations</note> or the Koti-homa <note>a sacrifice of 10,000,000 oblations</note> when Venus and Jupiter are powerful. The constellations of Anuradha, Ashwini, Shatabhisha, Punarvasu, Rohini, Mula, Uttara Bhadrapada, Dhanishta, and Revati are praised for the performance of fire-oblations, auspicious sacrifices, and rites intended to increase wealth, grain, and children. **Now, the Wheel of Fire.** <note>term: *Agni-chakra*; a diagram used to determine if the "Fire" is on Earth (safe for rituals) or in the Heavens/Underworld</note>

<summary>This page concludes the rules for traveling in the direction of Venus (Pratishukra), noting lineage-based exceptions, and introduces the auspicious lunar mansions and planetary conditions for Agnyadhana (establishing sacred fires) and grand sacrifices.</summary>

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion on the timing of journeys and transitions from the completion of the marriage rituals into the protocols for royal consecration and the specific astrological influences of the planet Mars.</meta>

In the same way, the positions of Mercury, Jupiter, Saturn, and the Moon should be considered. When Rahu <note>original: *vidhuntuda*; the ascending node of the moon</note>, Ketu <note>the descending node</note>, and the Sun are in certain alignments, they may cause a "day-break" <note>an inauspicious astrological interference</note>. One should calculate in groups of three starting from the Sun. Those markers associated with the Sun, the "cruel" planets <note>malefics like Mars or Saturn</note>, and Ketu are like consuming fire and are not auspicious; only the others grant blessings. This **Fire Wheel** <note>original: *dahana-chakra*</note> should be carefully observed in all human endeavors to ensure total success. 

**Now, the Residence of Fire:**
According to the teachings of the sage Vashishtha, take the number of the lunar day <note>original: *tithi*</note>, add the number of the day of the week, and multiply the sum by four. If the result <note>when divided</note> indicates that fire resides in the heavens or the water <note>the underworld</note>, it is inauspicious. However, when fire resides on the Earth, it brings fulfillment. <unclear>vidanti</unclear> When the fire is properly situated, the ritual union is successful; otherwise, if fire is misplaced, it leads to the destruction of the work and the person should not proceed with the rites.

### Thus ends the Chapter on the Giving of a Bride in the "Mirror of Auspicious Moments"
<note>original: *iti muhurta-darpane kanyadana prakaranam*</note>

### Now, the Coronation
<note>original: *abhisheka*; the ritual sprinkling of water used to consecrate a king or a deity</note>

The coronation is auspicious when the Moon is in the following lunar mansions <note>original: *nakshatras*</note>: Revati, the three "Uttaras" <note>Uttara Phalguni, Uttara Ashadha, and Uttara Bhadrapada</note>, Anuradha, Pushya, Shravana, Jyeshtha, Ashvini, Rohini, and Punarvasu. It should take place when the Sun is in a favorable sign, the Moon is in the "Trines" <note>the 5th or 9th houses</note>, the benefics are in a good state, and the malefics are in the "Growing Houses" <note>the 3rd, 6th, or 11th houses</note>. The preferred days of the week are Wednesday, Thursday, and Friday. As stated in the *Gems of Poetry* <note>original: *Ratnamala*</note>, the coronation of kings is best when the lord of the rising sign and the lord of the current planetary period are strong. When the Sun, Mercury, and Venus are powerful, the coronation of the lords of the earth is desired. 

The sage Maheshvara adds: A coronation should not be performed during the dark fortnight <note>the waning moon</note>, nor in an intercalary month <note>original: *adhimasa*; an extra month added to the lunar calendar</note>, nor while Lord Vishnu is in his ritual sleep <note>the four-month period of *Chaturmasya*</note>, nor on "Empty" lunar days <note>original: *rikta*; the 4th, 9th, and 14th days of the lunar cycle</note>, nor at night.

### Now, the Chapter on Mars from "The Valour of Bhima"
<note>original: *atha bhima-parakrame bhauma-prakaranam*</note>

For the consecration of a kingdom, one should avoid the day of Mars. However, the sages say that the day of the "Son of the Earth" <note>original: *maatanaya*; a common epithet for the planet Mars</note> is perfect for beginning activities involving fire, battle, or logic and debate, as it ensures success in these endeavors. Sage Kashyapa notes: The three "Uttaras"...

**Now, the Ascendant:**
The sage <term>Vashishtha</term> <note>a legendary Vedic seer and authoritative figure in Sanskrit astrological and legal literature</note> states: When the trines and quadrants <note>original: *trikona-kendra*; the most powerful houses in a birth chart (1, 4, 5, 7, 9, 10)</note> are occupied by benefic planets, and the 3rd, 6th, and 11th houses are occupied by malefics, and provided the 6th, 8th, and 12th houses <note>known as the 'dusthana' or difficult houses</note> are empty, the coronation of a king brings auspicious results.

**Now, Journeys:**
The sage <term>Vashishtha</term> describes the seventh day of Venus and other astrological faults related to Venus which hinder the travels of men. Regarding the King's victory-expedition, the astrologer speaks:

**Purity of the Lunar Day:**
The sage <term>Narada</term> <note>the divine messenger and sage of the gods</note> states regarding the purity of the lunar days <note>original: *tithi-shuddhi*</note>: The 6th, 8th, and 12th days, as well as the "Empty" days <note>original: *rikta*; the 4th, 9th, and 14th days of a lunar fortnight</note>, should be avoided. The first day of the fortnight <note>original: *pratipada*</note> leads to the loss of wealth rather than its gain.

**Purity of the Weekday:**
The sage <term>Vashishtha</term> states: The days and planetary hours <note>original: *vargas*</note> belonging to Jupiter, Venus, and Mercury are auspicious for setting out. The days and hours of the Sun, Mars, and Saturn are not auspicious for travel.

**Purity of the Lunar Mansions:**
The sage <term>Vashishtha</term> states: When the Moon is in Revati, Anuradha, Shravana, Pushya, Rohini, Ashvini, or Punarvasu, travel provides men with their desired fruits, provided all the stars are favorable. Magha, Vishakha, Swati, Bharani, Ardra, Krittika, and Jyeshtha are inauspicious for setting out; other mansions not mentioned here are acceptable, as they are not specifically condemned.

**Lunar Mansions for All Directions:**
The mansions of Pushya, Shravana, Ashvini, and Anuradha are suitable for travel in any direction; they grant the fulfillment of all desires and wealth.

The sage <term>Narada</term> states: On the days of the Sun or Friday, the eastern direction...

Regarding the southern and northern directions: the Teacher <note>original: *Guru*; likely referring to a specific preceptor or Brihaspati</note> describes the remedy <note>original: *parihara*</note> for avoiding pitfalls in those directions and for the western and northern regions. He discusses [the preparation of] betel leaves with sandalwood... [and matters related to] the intake of food.

**Now, the Results of the Lunar Mansions according to Shripati:**
<note>Shripati was a prominent 11th-century Indian astronomer and mathematician</note>
In the mansions of Jyeshtha, Bharani, and Ardra, one should not [travel] toward the west. Regarding the regions of Phalguni, Uttara, and Mula, one should mark the specific divisions... concerning the influence of Venus <note>original: *Shukre*</note>. The sage **Vashishtha** states: Those mansions known as "Mula" <note>the 'root' stars</note> and Pushya... those who are journeying <note>original: *yayino*</note> should be wise and knowledgeable of these matters.

The sage **Narada** describes the remedy: one should sequentially mark the gateways... starting from the direction of the fire-spirit <note>the South-East</note>...

### Now, the Division of the "Living Side" and the "Dead Side"
<note>original: *atha jiva-paksha mritya-paksha*; a method of dividing the 27 lunar mansions into two groups based on the position of Rahu to determine if a time is "alive" with potential or "dead" and dangerous</note>

At the time of sunrise, the lunar mansions <note>original: *nakshatras*</note> consumed by the shadow-planet **Rahu** <term>Rahu</term> <note>the celestial deity responsible for eclipses, representing the ascending node of the moon</note> are calculated. Thirteen mansions are assigned to the **Living Side**, and thirteen are proclaimed as the **Dead Side**. One should indicate where Rahu is situated in the "mouth" [of the cycle]. 

The results are as follows: if the Sun <note>original: *Ravau*</note> is in the **Living Side** at that time, the journey is auspicious; if it is in the opposite side, it results in loss. When both the Moon and the Sun are joined and situated in the **Living Side**, victory and profit will undoubtedly be attained at the time of the journey. If, however, the Sun is situated in the **Dead Side**, the results of the journey are considered to be loss, fear, and death.

**Now, the Consideration of Akala:**
<note>original: *akala*; refers to an "untimely" or inauspicious astrological period</note>
The second, tenth, and sixth [lunar days] are declared as Akala...

...all are filled with patience; the remaining are the most excellent arts. Now, regarding the days of the week: **The Sun** <note>original: *Ravi*</note>, **The Moon** <note>original: *Chandra*</note>, **Jupiter** <note>original: *Guru*</note>, and **Saturn** <note>original: *Sauri*</note> are considered to belong to the "Common" <term>Akula</term> <note>literally "not of a specific family"; in this context, it refers to a category of time suitable for outward movement</note> group.

**Mars** <note>original: *Bhauma*</note> and **Venus** <note>original: *Shukra*</note> are also "Common," while **Mercury** <note>original: *Budha*</note> is considered "Noble" <term>Kula</term> <note>literally "of the family"; usually refers to stable or inward-looking times</note>.

### The Classification of Lunar Mansions by Shripati
<note>Shripati was a renowned 11th-century authority on astral science</note>

The lunar mansions <note>original: *nakshatras*</note> associated with the Fire-spirit <note>Krittika</note>, the Moon <note>Rohini</note>, the Lord of Creatures <note>Mrigashira</note>, and the Fathers <note>Magha</note>... as well as the mansion of the Physicians <note>Ashwini</note> and the Returning Wealth <note>Punavasu</note>, are clearly designated as "Noble" <note>original: *kulaja*</note>.

The mansions of the Sustainer <note>Pushya</note>, the Goat-born <note>Purva Bhadrapada</note>, and the Foot of the Goat <note>Uttara Bhadrapada</note> are "Common" <note>Akula</note>. Thus, the Noble and Common categories have been declared. For the remaining mansions and days: 

**Victory** belongs to the traveler who sets out during "Common" <note>Akula</note> times. 
**Loss** belongs to the traveler who sets out during "Noble" <note>Kula</note> times. 
A "Noble-Common" junction <note>original: *kula-akula*</note> results in a stalemate or a treaty <note>original: *sandhi*</note>.

### The Fruit of the Rising Yoginis
<note>The Yoginis are eight goddesses who inhabit different directions depending on the lunar day (tithi). A traveler must know their location to ensure they do not face the goddess directly, which brings bad luck.</note>

Now, the results of the **Yoginis**:
On the 1st and 9th lunar days, the goddess **Brahmi** rises in the **East**.
On the 2nd and 10th lunar days, **Maheshvari** <note>the Great Goddess</note> is in the **North**.
On the 3rd and 11th lunar days, **Kaumari** <note>the Maiden Goddess</note> resides in the **South-East** <note>original: *trikonaga* or "the triangle"</note>.
On the 4th and 12th lunar days, **Vaishnavi** is in the **South-West**.
On the 5th and 13th lunar days, **Varahi** <note>the Sow-headed Goddess</note> is in the **South**.
On the 6th and 14th lunar days, **Indrani** rises in the **West**.
On the 7th and the Full Moon <note>15th</note>, **Chandika** rises in the **North-West**.
On the 8th and the New Moon <note>30th</note>, **Mahalakshmi** <note>the Goddess of Fortune</note> resides in the **North-East** <note>original: *shivalaye* or "the abode of Shiva"</note>.

These are the positions of the **Yoginis** in sequence starting from the 1st day. When moving toward the South or elsewhere, the traveler should consider these positions carefully. 

*   **In War:** The **Yoginis** should always be to the **Left** or to the **Front** to grant victory. 
*   **For Travel or Entry:** At the time of a journey <note>original: *yatra*</note> or entering a city, one must avoid having the **Yogini** directly in **Front**. 

### The Noose of Time
<term>Kalapasha</term> <note>A specific astrological calculation used to determine "deadly" hours of the day or night</note>
Regarding the **Noose of Time**: In the reverse order... during the day, these are calculated starting from the Sun. At night, one should calculate these in reverse...

...**Victo**ry <note>continuing from "Victory" on the previous page</note> and success in travel or war are denied if one faces the opposing force directly; such a position must be avoided. Now, the three types of royal power are described in the *Essence of Battle* <note>original: *Samarasara*, a classical Sanskrit treatise on military astrology and omens</note> as belonging to Death, Shiva, and the Bearer of the Noose <note>Varuna</note>. These are represented by the numbers 4 and 2, and the color red.

In the divisions of the day, the directions for victory associated with Indra's noose are governed by the Moon and Mercury... <unclear>illegible</unclear>... these directions are given as auspicious. For those seeking victory, knowledge of the ten divisions of the day is essential. The Western direction is governed by the noose of Indra; for water, the South; for knowledge, the West; and for the Southeast, the remaining directions. These positions remain for the duration of a watch <term>prahara</term> <note>a traditional unit of time lasting approximately three hours</note>.

### The Fatal Lunar Days
<term>Ghata-tithi</term> <note>literally "striking lunar day"; a specific date in the lunar month considered dangerous or unlucky for a particular zodiac sign</note>

For the Bull <note>Taurus</note> and the Virgin <note>Virgo</note>, the *Bhadra* <note>the 2nd, 7th, or 12th lunar days</note> is remembered as the fatal lunar day. For the Crab <note>Cancer</note>, it is *Ananda*; for the Scorpion <note>Scorpio</note>, it is *Jaya*; and for the Horned Goat <note>Capricorn</note>, it is the day of the gods. In these times, wealth and auspiciousness are not obtained.

### The Fatal Weekdays
<term>Ghata-vara</term> <note>specific days of the week that are inauspicious for certain zodiac signs</note>

The following days are considered "striking" or inauspicious for the respective signs:
*   **Tuesday** <note>original: *Bhauma*</note> is fatal for the Horned Goat <note>Capricorn</note>.
*   The Bull <note>Taurus</note>, the Lion <note>Leo</note>, and the Virgin <note>Virgo</note> have their own specific fatal days.
*   **Friday** <note>original: *Shukra*</note> is fatal for the Crab <note>Cancer</note>.
*   **Thursday** <note>original: *Jiva*</note> is fatal for the Fish <note>Pisces</note> and the Water-Pot <note>Aquarius</note>.
These fatal weekdays are not recommended for starting any significant action.

### The Fatal Lunar Mansions
<term>Ghata-nakshatra</term> <note>the specific star signs among the 27 mansions that act as "strikers" of bad luck for an individual</note>

The fatal mansions include: The Great One <note>Magha</note>, the Sun-star <note>Hasta</note>, the Independent One <note>Swati</note>, the Friendly One <note>Anuradha</note>, the Hearing <note>Shravana</note>, the Veiler <note>Shatabhisha</note>, the Wealthy <note>Revati</note>, the Restrainer <note>Bharani</note>, and the Supporter <note>Rohini</note>. These are calculated starting from the Ram <note>Aries</note>.

### The Fatal Piercing
<term>Ghata-vedha</term> <note>an astrological "obstruction" where planets in certain positions cancel out the benefits of others</note>

The nature and number of these obstructions, such as the "Thunderbolt" <note>Vajra</note> and others, should be understood in sequence starting from the Ram <note>Aries</note> for the purposes of travel and war.

### The Fatal Moon
<term>Ghata-chandra</term> <note>a transit of the Moon into a specific house relative to one's birth sign that is considered dangerous</note>

In battle, in legal disputes, in the worship of the Maiden Goddess, in service to a King, and when first mounting a vehicle, one must avoid the Fatal Moon.

### The Universal Number
<term>Sarvanka</term> <note>a mathematical formula used to determine the overall potential for success or failure</note>

The wise astrologer should combine the numbers associated with the lunar day, the weekday, and the lunar mansion. This sum should be multiplied or divided by the specific numerical codes: 3 <note>Rama</note>, 4 <note>the Vedas</note>, 6 <note>the limbs of the Veda</note>, 8, and 7 <note>the Sages</note>, and finally by 3 <note>the Fires</note>. 

*   If the **beginning** of the calculation results in a zero: The outcome will be sharp or piercing.
*   If the **middle** results in a zero: There will be great fear.
*   If the **end** results in a zero: The result will be hardship and suffering.
*   If the Universal Number is complete: Victory shall be achieved.

Now, the Tatal Yoga <note>a specific planetary combination</note> part 1...

### Calculations for Success and Travel
Take the degree of the Sun and the degree of the specific sign <note>original: *sūryabhāṅgatvayoḥ*; likely referring to the Sun’s position and the ascendant</note>, then divide by seven. The remainders—one, three, five, and four—are determined by the depths of the calculation <note>original: *haratalena*</note>. These certainly indicate success in one's ventures. Whether in travel, or in the flow of battle <note>original: *samara*</note>, or in the pursuit of wealth, or in the digging of wells and ponds, or the construction of palaces, or in the mounting of horses and elephants—these calculations must be used. One who knows the science should always avoid the flaws <note>original: *doshān*</note> in auspicious rituals to ensure prosperity.

Multiply the degrees of the Sun and the degrees of the sign by two, add the lunar date <term>tithi</term> and the day of the week <term>vāra</term>, then divide the sum by nine. This application is famously proclaimed as the "Himalayan method" <note>original: *himavattat-prakīrtitaṃ*</note>.

**<term>Garga</term>:** <note>Garga is one of the most ancient legendary seers of Indian astronomy and astrology</note>
If both entry <note>returning home</note> and departure <note>starting a journey</note> are known to occur on the very same day, the wise should consider that an "entering journey." In such a case, one should not contemplate a long expedition.

**<term>Bhrigu</term>:** <note>Another foundational sage of the Vedic tradition, associated with the planet Venus</note>
If a journey and a return occur on the same day, one should not worry about the "Spike" <note>original: *shūla*; an inauspicious direction for travel on certain days</note> or the position of Venus. One should also not worry about the <term>yoginī</term> <note>female spirits whose directional position affects the luck of a journey</note> or the "Jewel-Creeper" <note>original: *ratnavalyā*</note> when returning home.

If a journey begins at the rising of the Sun or during the Moon's phase, or even when one's strength is failing <note>original: *ashaktiḥ*</note>, a journey that has already commenced is praised by the ancients.

**<term>Garga</term>:**
Venus is the eye, but Jupiter is the ultimate benefit. When a Brahmin speaks words of encouragement, it is as if the god Janardana <note>Vishnu</note> himself has spoken. This is the view of <term>Varāhamihira</term> <note>a 6th-century polymath and astrologer</note>: victory is certain when the planets are favorably placed.

### Travel by Water
The cruel planets <note>such as Mars or Saturn</note> produce bad results, while the gentle planets <note>the benefics, like Jupiter or Venus</note> bring auspicious fruit if they have not set <note>become invisible behind the Sun</note>. In the "Jewel-Creeper" arrangement, the Fish (Pisces) and the Water-Pot (Aquarius) are considered empty and bring fear. However, the Crab (Cancer), the Bow (Sagittarius), and the Fish (Pisces) are praised for water-borne journeys, especially when a watery sign is rising at the horizon. When the Crab (Cancer), the Water-Insect (Scorpio), the Fish (Pisces), or the Bull (Taurus) are rising, the journey will be successful.

**<term>Lagna</term>:** <note>the Ascendant, or the sign rising on the eastern horizon at the moment of an event</note>
For any journey by water, it is recommended that the rising sign be a "watery" sign and that it be occupied by auspicious planets.

**<term>Vasiṣṭha</term>:** <note>A celebrated Vedic sage and author of astrological treatises</note>
When the birth sign <note>the sign the Moon occupied at birth</note> is in the Ascendant, or when the lord of that sign is in the... <unclear>illegible</unclear>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for journey astrology (Yatra-shastra), transitioning from general timings to specific planetary configurations (yogas) that ensure success or warn of danger from thieves and enemies. It references the authority of Varahamihira, one of the most celebrated astronomers in Indian history.</meta>

->Propitiousness<-

A journey that grants one's desired results <note>original: *abhīṣṭaphaladā-yātrā*</note> should be undertaken quickly to dispel fear. If the journey begins when the Ascendant <term>Lagna</term> is in its own sign or a favorable sign, it is successful; however, if the Ascendant is weak or falls in the twelfth house, the journey will meet with failure <note>original: *yāta-bhaṅgo*, literally "breaking of the march"</note>. The "Directional Gates" <note>original: *dig-dvārāṇayaḥ*</note> for the East and other cardinal points follow the order of the zodiac signs starting from Aries <term>Mesha</term>. All such gates, when properly aligned, bestow auspiciousness. When the Ascendant of the "Directional Gate" matches the purpose of the journey, it brings victory. However, if the Lord of the Direction <term>Dikpati</term> is in an unfavorable or reverse position in the Ascendant, it brings loss and fear of enemies.

Regarding the "Eightfold Forehead Configuration" <note>original: *aṣṭa-lalāṭa-yoga*</note>, **Varahamihira** states: If the stars are positioned in the house of the enemy at the time of the Ascendant, or if the Son of the Sun <note>original: *dinakara-sutaḥ*; the planet Saturn</note> is in the fourth house <term>Patala</term>, there is "divine friendship" <note>original: *deva-maitrī*</note>. But if Venus or the Sun <note>original: *bhāskara*</term> is in an undignified position, or if Rahu is in the seventh house, it leads to destruction and death. Such configurations ward off the benefits of the journey.

Through these combinations, the presence of robbers and thieves <note>original: *taskara-caura-ādayaḥ*</note encountered in the past can be understood. By the strength of the lunar mansions <term>Nakshatra</term>, one's relatives and the intensity of a situation are known. If the Ascendant is purified and powerful, then even if there were thieves before, they will be replaced by the "sounds of horses" <note>original: *aśva-ghoṣā*; a metaphor for a royal or military procession</note> and victory will be attained. Again, if the Lord of the Ascendant—whether Jupiter, Mercury, Venus, or Saturn—is in the tenth or second house, and the Moon is also well-placed, the traveler will surely obtain their desired fruits. By this planetary combination <term>yoga</term>, kings who set out against their enemies will find safety and witness the destruction of those enemies. Without such a combination, victory over the earth is not achieved.

If even one of Mercury <note>original: *Budha*</note>, Jupiter <note>original: *Bṛhaspati*</note>, or Venus <note>original: *Bhārgava*</note> is placed in the ninth, fifth, or in the cardinal angles <term>Kendra</term>, the sages call it a favorable combination. If two of these planets are so placed, it is called "Adhi-yoga." If three are present, others say it is the most supreme Adhi-yoga. **Varahamihira** states that this brings welfare, the appointment of ministers, the birth of progeny, and the favor of gods and priests <term>Brahmin</term>.

<keywords>Varahamihira, Yatra-shastra, Lagna (Ascendant), Dig-dwara (Directional Gates), Adhi-yoga, astrological timing, protective charms, thieves, royal victory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for journey astrology, specifically detailing the ritual of formal departure (Prasthana) and the distances to be covered. It transitions from the planetary configurations discussed on the previous page to the specific conduct required of a king and the various distances recommended by ancient authorities like Garga and Vasishtha.</meta>

<margin>Mu.
Ha. <note>likely abbreviations for scribal references or manuscript families</note></margin>
One should desire to follow the example of King Janaka. After walking thirty-two steps <note>original: *dvātriṃśattamamupagamya-padāni*</note> upon the ground toward the East, or toward the direction of the city, chariots, or horses, one should then proceed. 
<margin>19
19</margin>
According to **Garga**, the king should stay at the house of a relative or at a house just beyond the boundary. **Bhrigu** advises staying just beyond the border. **Bharadwaja** suggests staying in the autumn quarters, while **Vasishtha** recommends staying outside the city. **Sripati** states that the king should reside in one place for no more than nine days, and the regional lords <note>original: *maṇḍaleśāḥ*</note> should not stay for more than seven. Even an ordinary person should not stay for more than five nights if there is a breach in the journey's timing. 

On the first day of the march, it is praised to travel a certain distance. After traveling two *yojanas* <term>yojana; a Vedic measure of distance, roughly 8-9 miles</term>, the Lord of the Earth <note>the King</note> should take up residence. On the third day, he should stay at a distance of three and a half *yojanas*. Thereafter, the Lord of the Earth should proceed along the path as seen or customary. 

The authorities speak of the first stage of the journey: after advancing twenty or thirty steps, the king—having abandoned any "difficult vehicle" <note>referring to an inauspicious carriage or mode of transport</note>—undertakes the journey. After giving a vessel of gold to the **best of astrologers** <term>ganaka-varāya</term>, he should perform the **formal departure** <term>prasthāna</term>. 

Regarding the rules of **Garga**: A king who has formally set out and commenced his journey should not turn back, nor should he treat the departure lightly; to do so would be to forfeit his very life. Whether it be his own people or others, or whether he has struck a woman or a man <note>implying an accidental or intentional conflict at the moment of leaving</note>, he must continue; for that journey is now his life. 

One who is intoxicated, diseased, terrified, exhausted, angry, or insulted should not proceed with the departure. One should stand upon the path according to the sacred scriptures and the methods of the Threefold Veda <note>original: *trivṛta*</note>, maintaining a mind fixed on the royal umbrella and the mountain Meru. 

At the time of the **formal departure** <term>prasthāna</term>, a man should always avoid sexual intercourse <term>maithuna</term>. The wise man seeking victory should also avoid **shaving** <term>vapana</term>, **purgation** <term>vamana</term>, **oil massage** <term>tailā-bhyaṅga</term>, and applying eye-ointment <note>original: *ulocane*</note> at the time of the journey. If a man indulges in sexual intercourse when the time for duty <note>the journey</note> has arrived, he will return afflicted by disease, anger, and a loss of wealth. He should also avoid the smell of pungent oils...

<summary>This page outlines the ritual protocols for a king's formal departure (Prasthana), citing authorities like Garga and Vasishtha on where to camp and what distances to cover. It strictly forbids certain activities like shaving, oil massage, and sex during the initiation of a journey to ensure success and health.</summary>

--- Page 43 ---
<meta>This page continues the specific protocols for the journey (Yatra) of a king or traveler. The previous page concluded with the initial steps of the departure (Prasthana) and the distances to be covered. This section transitions into the behavioral taboos during travel, specific auspicious locations for making camp as cited from the Rajamartanda, and a detailed examination of "unseasonal rain" as an astrological impediment.</meta>

If one travels after eating, or sets out while confused or suffering from illness, one should return. The sage **Atri** says: "One who, having reached the destination of their journey, does not cherish or greet his wife—or if he greets the wife [improperly/at the wrong time]—he should diligently return." Similarly, one who has reached the journey’s destination but then returns home again, whether out of desire or some other purpose, shall not attain success in his undertaking.

In the **Rajamartanda** <note>original: *Rajamartanda*; a famous 11th-century encyclopedic work on astrology and law attributed to King Bhoja</note>, there are special rules: holding an umbrella, being in a state of intoxication, or urinating [are prohibited during the ritual start]. The twice-born <term>dvija; a member of the three upper social classes—Priests, Warriors, or Merchants—who has undergone the sacred thread ceremony</term> should look upon their own sacred thread or garment after placing it upon a tree. 

A formal departure <note>original: *prasthana*</note> for a journey is said to be auspicious if it occurs near a village, a river, the house of a Brahman, a temple, in a forest, by the bank of a pond, or in any place that is naturally pleasing to the mind.

One should not set out on a journey during one's birth star <term>janma-nakshatra; the specific lunar mansion the moon occupied at the moment of one's birth</term>, nor on the eighth lunar day. Furthermore, one should not depart on a Tuesday <note>original: *bhauma*</note> or a Saturday <note>original: *shani*</note>; a journey started on these censured days will not bring satisfaction.

The **Rajamartanda** states that seven days are auspicious for traveling toward the East; five days are very auspicious for the South; three days for the West; and for leaders, two days are recommended for departures toward the North. 

At the start, after first seeing a full pitcher <note>original: *purna kumbha*; a ritual vessel filled with water, symbolizing abundance and success</note>, one should not then proceed with fire. [The text mentions a ritual involving] immersion of the feet in cow dung <note>original: *gomaya*</note>... If cow dung is seen smeared or raised, it indicates the Mandavi ritual observation.

Regarding the prohibition of unseasonal rain <term>akaala vrishti; rain that falls outside the predictable monsoon season, often interpreted as a divine warning or bad omen</term>, it is known in the **Rajamartanda** that from the month of Margashirsha <note>roughly November–December</note> onwards, the sages **Vyasa**, **Valmiki**, and **Garga** state that rain is considered a "fault" or defect until the completion of the Tonsure vows <note>original: *chuda vrata*</note>. 

When Jupiter <note>original: *guru*</note>, the lord of the gods, is retrograde in this period, these months do not yield auspicious fruits. The four months starting from Pausha <note>roughly December–January</note> are declared to be the time of unseasonal rain. One should avoid journeys <note>original: *yatra*</note> and staying abroad during this time, particularly if rain falls on the full moon of the month of Margashirsha.

<summary>This page outlines behavioral prohibitions for travelers, auspicious locations for starting a journey according to King Bhoja’s Rajamartanda, and astrological warnings regarding unseasonal rain during the winter months.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the specific behavioral and ritual codes for a traveler or king preparing for a journey. While the previous page focused on the physical starting point and astrological timing (nakshatras), this page delves into the moral and physical purity required before departure, specifically regarding marital relations and ritual impurity, before transitioning into a comprehensive list of auspicious omens (shakuna) that signify a successful mission.</meta>

<note>Pencil note:</note> 20

...knowing the time of the [ascendant] and the nature of rain in the months of Magha and Phalguna <note>mid-January to mid-March</note>, as known in the **Code of Conduct** <note>original: *Vivahara Samhita*; a treatise on social and ritual laws</note>, there are rules for departure <note>original: *prasthana-niyama*</note>. 

One should avoid [intercourse] three times. On the days of women and cows, and similarly during solar rites, one should abstain. In the same way, one should avoid sexual union <term>maithuna; ritual or physical sexual intercourse</term> for seven nights [before a journey]. If a man approaches his wife for intercourse on the night or morning after she has taken her ritual bath following menstruation <term>rtusnata; a woman who has completed her menstrual period and undergone a purifying bath</term>, he shall not attain the fruit of his journey and will return in a state of distress. 

As long as he intends to travel or is focused on his mission, his mind should remain disciplined regarding union. However, if a husband does not approach his wife when she is in her fertile period after the ritual bath, he incurs the sin of theft and the killing of a priest <note>original: *brahma-hatya*; one of the greatest sins in ancient Indian ethics</note>; of this, there is no doubt. 

Regarding the **Shravana** <note>an auspicious lunar mansion or a specific ritual context</note>: if one sets out after having performed a successful union and a purifying bath, being joyful in spirit, he shall attain his heart's desires in a short time. 

The sage **Narada** says: "One who desires a long life should not undertake a journey during the northern course of the sun <term>uttarayana; the six-month period when the sun moves north, generally considered auspicious</term> if the rites of consecration, purification, or the impurity of birth/death <term>sutaka; ritual impurity following a birth or death in the family</term> are not yet completed."

### Now, the Omens
<note>original: *Atha Shakunani*</note>

The following sounds are considered auspicious for departure: the *bheri* drum, the *mridanga* <note>a double-headed barrel drum</note>, the *muraja* drum, the blowing of a conch shell, the flute, the lute <term>vina; a traditional stringed instrument</term>, the sound of Vedic chanting, and the echoes of sweet, auspicious songs. 

The following sights are praised as excellent omens: a man accompanied by his son, a beautiful young woman, a fragrant cow accompanied by her calf, and someone wearing freshly washed white garments. 

Further signs of success in one’s purpose include: a golden pitcher, collyrium <note>eye ointment</note>, a mare, a person carrying sugar, a banner, a lamp, a vessel filled with water, or a full pot <term>purna-kalasha; a traditional symbol of abundance and source of life</term>. Success is also indicated by the sight of a lute, a flag, a fish, a lone elephant, a chariot, curds, a conch shell, yellow pigment <term>go-rochana; a bright yellow pigment used for tilaks, derived from a cow</term>, a maiden, and especially the sight of two priests <term>vipra; Brahmins</term> moving toward each other. 

A corpse, if it has been ritually bathed and is being carried toward the entrance, is surprisingly considered a giver of success <note>This is a common paradox in Indian omen-lore (shakuna-shastra) where certain "in-auspicious" sights are auspicious if encountered in a specific manner during departure.</note>.

The authority **Vasantaraja** <note>author of the *Vasantaraja Shakuna*, a famous 12th-century work on omens</note> states: "If someone seeking water goes out with an empty pot but meets someone else returning with great speed carrying a full one, [the traveler] should take that as a sign of success."

<meta>This page continues the transition from the internal state of the traveler to the external world, moving from the rules of physical and moral purity discussed on the previous page to the interpretation of external signs. ...[just as a traveler] returns successful in his mission, so too, as described in the **Light of Astrology** <note>original: *Jyotisprakasha*; a classic Sanskrit text on astrology and divination</note>, animals that appear while moving in a clockwise direction <term>pradakshina; a respectful circumambulation or movement to the right</term> are auspicious signs.

A donkey [heard or seen] on the left is auspicious. When deer or other animals behave in this way, they are favorable omens. However, in this world, certain signs indicate the interruption of a man’s desired goals: the sound of weeping, quarreling, a house on fire, the sight of a woman [in certain states], or cats fighting. Tripping or stumbling at the start of a journey, or the appearance of cuckoos, pigeons, or buffaloes at the time of departure, indicates that a person's intended purpose will be frustrated.

The following items are considered inauspicious if seen upon leaving:
- Cotton and medicines
- Salt
- Oil used for play or grooming
- Fat or marrow
- Mud, charcoal, or ashes
- Hides and leather
- Human hair or animal dung
- A person with a copper-colored body or a shaven head
- Someone frowning or with disheveled hair
- Those wearing ochre robes <note>original: *kashayina*; typically referring to ascetics or monks, whose sight was sometimes considered a bad omen for worldly missions involving wealth or battle</note>

<insert>Bad omens from the First Chapter of the Samhita</insert> 

If these sights are encountered, one must never proceed on a journey. One should remain where they are. If a bad omen occurs, a wealthy man or any traveler should turn back, wash his hands and feet, perform ritual sipping of water <term>acamya; a purification ritual involving sipping water from the palm</term>, or take a bath before looking at the omens again.

Upon witnessing the first bad omen, the traveler experiences a setback; after the second, a greater loss; and after the third, one should not even think of leaving the house. Crows, partridges, cats, and dogs are auspicious if they are seen on the left when entering a place, but on the right side when departing on a journey.

<insert>Now, the inquiry into sneezing</insert> 

When a bad omen is seen standing in one's way, one should give gold <note>original: *kanchana*</note> in charity; then, free from sorrow, he may proceed. Regarding the inquiry into sneezing: in this matter, the sage Gautama says that [certain sneezes] can even predict death...

### The Interpretation of Horse Omens

A horse that is too young, too old, or suffering from chronic nasal congestion <note>original: *pinasa*; a respiratory ailment in horses</note> is considered "fruitless" and brings no success to the journey. When a horse is fearful, hesitant, or has eyes filled with tears, it portends trouble for its master. If a horse is well-trained and humble but suddenly behaves out of character or resists being mounted, it indicates that the master’s fortune <term>Lakshmi; the goddess of wealth and prosperity, here representing good luck</term> is departing.

Furthermore, consider the physical movements of the animal: if it stumbles without cause, keeps its ears in a "distressed" position <note>likely flattened against the head, a sign of agitation</note>, or exhales long, hot sighs toward the earth, these are grave signs. If its gaze is unfocused or it acts as if it were in a dream, the journey should be reconsidered. 

If a horse begins to rub against anthills, shrubs, or trees of its own accord, or if it moves with an unusually hurried or erratic gait at the moment of departure, it creates a "thunderbolt path" <note>original: *vajra-marga*; a metaphorical path of destruction or sudden misfortune</note>. During such a time, one should not proceed. These rules are established in the **Travel Section of the Mirror of Auspicious Moments** <note>original: *Shani-muhurta-darpana*; a reference to a specialized astrological text</note>.

<insert>Now begins the Commencement of House Construction, according to Shripati and Vishnu</insert> 

### The Science of Architecture (Vastu Shastra)

When beginning the construction of a home, the timing is of utmost importance. The months determine the fate of the dwellers:

*   **Chaitra:** Starting in this month leads to grief and the loss of wealth <note>roughly March-April; often considered inauspicious for building as it marks the end of the traditional year</note>.
*   **Vaishakha:** Leads to the gain of gems and prosperity.
*   **Jyeshtha:** Results in fear or even death.
*   **Ashadha:** Leads to the loss of servants and livestock.
*   **Shravana:** Brings an abundance of wealth and the favor of **Lakshmi**.

It is taught in the **Science of Architecture** <term>Vastu Shastra; the traditional Indian system of architecture and design</term> that one obtains prosperity through the proper sequence of months. According to the view of the Great Teacher **Yogeshvara**, a king or a common man should only build a residence <note>original: *niketana*; a formal word for house or habitation</note> when the astrological alignments are favorable.
